All thirty-seven companies of Madras and Mysore together possessed net funds of only Rs. 5 crore and 86 lakh. Bombay's Oriental alone possessed Rs. 35 crore and 83 lakh, more than six times their combined wealth. Its managing figure was Sir Purshottamdas Thakurdas, one of the three men who prepared the Bombay Plan with Birla. Hindustan had Rs. 6 crore and 46 lakh; Empire and New India were likewise individually stronger than all thirty-seven southern companies together.

The ten listed northern companies alone possessed about Rs. 76 crore in net funds. After the required investment in government securities, the remainder could finance industry. This monetary government rested in the hands of Thakurdas, Mullick, Sarkar, Kaswala, Mehta, Harishankar, Desai, Dalmia, Ishwardas, Premchand and Setalvad. In their country factories arose without obstruction, protected by a financial cannon ready to secure markets for their goods.

Now compare the leading companies of Madras and Mysore:

| Company | Net funds |
|---|---:|
| United India | Rs. 2 crore 18 lakh |
| Andhra | Rs. 57 lakh |
| Pandian | Rs. 42 lakh |
| Asiatic | Rs. 32 lakh |
| Indian Mutual | Rs. 26 lakh |
| South Indian Cooperative | Rs. 20 lakh |
| Prithvi | Rs. 16 lakh |
| New Guardian | Rs. 14 lakh |
| Postal | Rs. 10 lakh |
| Mangalore | Rs. 10 lakh |

The first northern company had Rs. 35 crore and 83 lakh. The figures then descended through six, five and four crores, while even the tenth had Rs. 2 crore and 42 lakh. The largest southern company began at only Rs. 2 crore and 18 lakh, below the tenth northern company. When the beginning itself is crooked, nothing need be said of those that follow.

A researcher once fed rats the diets eaten in Punjab, Kashmir and the Frontier, Bihar, Bengal, Rajasthan, Bombay, Madras and other regions. After several months, the Madras rat was the weakest and lightest. Our backwardness is not confined to food and living standards; it extends to industry. From rats to insurance companies, Madras struggles without strength, while the great rats that hide and carry away our goods grow fat because their burrows are in the North.

Our desire is that Madras should stand upright economically, not necessarily exceeding the North but no longer low enough to be exploited. Banks and insurance must gather scattered money and establish factories. Goods will increase, money regain value, and the people's lives flourish. But industrial development must not sacrifice the public to private domination. Neither Raja Sir, Alagappa nor Chidambaram should privately control prosperous banking and insurance; the Government must operate them. Wasted money can become employment for the jobless and goods to abolish famine.

Capital and need have increased, but remain unused. This is a land full of workers without factories, rich in natural resources whose use has been obstructed.

Palani's fertile southern field lies barren while the Bania's northern field flourishes. This cannot continue. Either the southern economy will be mortgaged to the financially powerful Bania like land surrendered on lease and be sucked by leeches, or the country itself will be handed over while its people perish as slaves in foreign sugar fields, rubber plantations, tea estates and mines. They cannot live in their birthplace: poverty amid abundance. The condition between disease and death cannot endure.

### In the Airways!

There are nineteen privately owned companies for civil aviation, with authorized capital of forty crore rupees. Tata and Dalmia operate aviation companies. Their income and growth steadily increase.

Why should these companies remain in private hands? Would it not be better and more profitable for the Government to operate them? Aviation and commerce possess a subtle connection. A merchant may have to reach Colombo before evening or lose several lakhs. He must travel by air. But suppose the aviation business belongs to another merchant connected with the same urgent transaction, and the passenger is told there is no seat. What then?

If the Government operated aviation, facilities would be equally available and profits would go to the public treasury rather than private capitalists. Government is also better placed than individuals to import machinery and experts. Yet it does not operate the service; private capitalists run the companies with forty crore rupees in capital. Asked why it has not taken over aviation, the Government replies, “It is not presently possible.”

### The New Imperialism

When other peoples feared to cross the sea, the valiant Tamil sailed abroad and traded with distant lands such as Yavana, as literature records.

What do present statistics say? While the Tamil stands with folded hands reading the tale of Rama's messenger crossing the sea, northern merchants cast their nets everywhere and accumulate profit.

Their financial and industrial power, fortified by political influence, reveals how rapidly a new economic imperialism is taking shape. Northerners have become industrial monarchs here, guardians of the money garden, and the dominant force in maritime commerce.

From the land of the seafaring Tamil, plantation labourers now depart for Mauritius. Who are the merchants exporting our goods and importing foreign goods? Predominantly northerners. They have established camps and brought export-import trade under their control. Few Tamils of consequence remain in this influential field connecting us with the world.

We provide a sample of northern names to show where our wealth has been mortgaged. Congress Tamils should ask whether Dravidian merchants possess even a thousandth of comparable influence in the North. Rupchands, Jivanlals, Shivnarayans and Mulchands operate from Madras and trade everything from groundnuts to diamonds. Do Dhanapal, Annamalai or Arumugam possess any such place in the North?

Read the source's list of ninety northern maritime merchants conducting business worth crores from within Dravida. Then place before your mind both the Tamil whose body is worn away in a tea plantation and the Marwari, Gujarati or Multani merchant who came south to prosper. Decide whether the principle “Dravida Nadu for Dravidians” is necessary.

### Ten Crores!

A new Bombay company was formed under the name National Rayon Corporation Limited. This was a great factory making many varieties of cloth, not a spinning wheel in every home: it would manufacture artificial silk yarn and weave garments from it.

Its capital was ten crore rupees. All raw materials required for rayon were available within India, eliminating dependence upon foreign sources. Two American companies were engaged to advise upon machinery and construction. Their experts would advise the company for ten years in return for payment: precisely the joint dominance of American and northern capital described earlier.

The nine directors included Sir Purshottamdas Thakurdas, Sir Ardeshir Dalal, Walchand Hirachand, Sir Manilal Nanavati, Navinchandra Mafatlal, Kasturbhai Lalbhai, A. D. Shroff, Jivanlal Chunilal Chinai and Rasiklal Jivanlal Chinai. Except for Kasturbhai Lalbhai of Ahmedabad, all were Bombay capitalists; all were northern.

The head office and managing agents were in Bombay. The factory was to be built at Kalyan near Bombay. Its bankers included Central Bank of India, Bank of Baroda, United Commercial Bank and National City Bank of New York; its legal advisers and auditors were likewise in Bombay. Ten crores, not even for natural silk but artificial silk. From the beginning it expected to manufacture ten tons of rayon yarn each day.

There, this. Here, “Spin, wheel, spin round and round!”

As factories built with crores grow there, khadi cannot remain their policy. Yet northern Congress ministries do not prohibit new mills. Bombay expands industrial wealth with crore upon crore while Madras is given cottage industry, spinning wheels and village methods of boiling jaggery. At the same time the Constituent Assembly contemplates placing Bombay and Madras in one group. What outcome is possible except making Madras Bombay's market?

### To Suppress the Revolution

A capitalist's commodity is created through labour, but its buyers need not be either capitalists or workers in that industry. There is a public outside the industry that purchases and uses its product.

The price paid by this public is divided into wages for labour and profit for capital. Industrial welfare depends upon whether that division is just. The buying public has as much right as employer or worker to discuss and reform it. Its opinion must therefore be heard.

People have long treated a railway strike or mill strike as somebody else's quarrel. That is wrong. The dispute concerns how employer and worker divide the money we paid. Since it is our money, we have every right to participate, state an opinion and help resolve the conflict. The public must say: “I buy the product and provide the money. I have the right to judge who should receive what share, because I supplied the sum being disputed.”

The Dravidar Kazhagam calls upon the public to recognize this right, pronounce upon justice and help settle disputes. It thereby creates a bond of affection between workers and the public. Other parties do not perform this task. A labour party and a capitalist party clash, while the Government enters claiming to preserve law, order and peace; no one recognizes that the issue should be brought before the public forum.

Comrades of the Dravidar Kazhagam, explain the reasons for the Coimbatore strike. Show the public how just the workers' demands are, and secure public affection and support for them. Whether they recognize it or refuse to, the comrades suffering in the mills are people of our land, Dravidians of our community.

This duty exists not only during one strike but whenever such occasions arise, and it must be undertaken with increasing clarity and commitment. The man beside the field may not know he is a Dravidian; he knows only that he is poor. We know both facts. To the mill worker, the Aryan-Dravidian issue may sound meaningless; he knows only his daily suffering. We know both. There is no wonder if they forget us; it would be an unforgivable crime for us to forget them. They do not know us, but we know them, and because we know them we shall serve them.

We know that the mill worker and field labourer may trust Kali or Mariamman more than Gandhi and may not accept our doctrine. Increased wages may merely enable him to break a few more coconuts at several more temples. But we must also know that wealthy men who accumulated money by striking the poor in the stomach gild the steps at Palani, waste it upon temple consecrations, and make offerings to pontiffs and gurus. They too do not spend upon reason or useful labour, but upon pleasure and hopes for a favourable afterlife.

Therefore, while working to return the poor man's share to him, we must not excuse the rich man's conduct by saying the poor will spend it uselessly. Wealth must reach the person who labours, and the wealth received must be spent usefully. Both are our principles; one must not be destroyed for the other. With one eye closed, an object cannot be seen correctly.

Recognizing this, the Dravidar Kazhagam attacks both social and economic disorder. This approach must grow more rapidly and clearly. Congress may sever the Communists' connection with labour through competition, propaganda or repression, but cutting does not destroy every root. Some plants exude sap when cut, fragrance when ground and more shoots when divided.

Even if the connection can be severed, removing it does not remove the problem. Long before the emergency law, we wrote that replacing Communists with Congressmen in the labour movement would not destroy the root. Once Congressmen worked directly upon labour problems, their white shirts would acquire saffron and then turn red: they too would become Communists.

The imprisonment of Congress member Narayanasamy of Tanjore supports this argument. Communists were arrested, and Congressmen too, because they appeared Communist and were thought to perform the same work. This will increasingly occur.

At the beginning of the Coimbatore strike, the mill owners used the familiar allegations made against Communists: that unionists created disputes, incited people, engaged in violence, intimidated and threatened. They even claimed these unionists behaved worse than Communists. *The Hindu* of 13 December 1947 quoted them: “It is most unfortunate that the Union leaders instead of creating a congenial atmosphere conducive to a settlement should be resorting to intimidation Violence and other tactics which are worse than those adopted by the Communists.” Whoever conducts a strike will face the same accusations.

Capitalists write the words “intimidation” and “violence” in large letters at the opening of every new account. The law attaches harsh punishment to them, and rulers are obliged to prevent such offences and enforce order. That is why these charges are kept permanently ready. It is meaningless to claim labour conflict worsens merely because one particular party works among labourers; whoever does so will face the same charge and action.

Congress Socialists are neither alleged traitors of August nor devotees of Moscow. They claim an international outlook capable of finding Stalin's errors while refusing to abandon Gandhism, and they criticize Communist methods. Yet what happens when they work among labourers? Suspicion falls even upon them. Jayaprakash Narayan reported two weeks earlier that many Congress Socialists had been arrested in Bihar.

Thus capitalism does not hesitate to accuse or punish anyone working among labourers, whether Congress, Congress Socialist or trade unionist. It is wrong to imagine that suppressing Communists will end labour unrest, or that only Communist labour work creates trouble while Congress or Socialist work will not. Only if leaders become counterfeit and reduce the labour movement to a grinning organization that begs for favours can they escape, performing a token exercise whose message is, “I shall strike without hurting you; you keep crying without stopping.” The moment they work sincerely, they are finished. Rulers will not embrace them merely because they are not Communists.

Capitalists openly say that Congress came to power through their favour. They therefore believe Congress ministries exist to secure capitalist domination, not to establish the rule of labour.

Under the previous Congress government, Congress Socialists were treated as Communists are treated now. Rajagopalachari issued a prohibitory order expelling the Congress Socialist Batliwala from Madras and mocked such men as hungry wanderers. Batliwala's offence occurred before August and could not be explained as betrayal of that movement. His error was adding the word “Socialist” to “Congress.” The scent of equality brought the rulers down upon him.

Capitalism and orthodoxy permit Congress only so far as it establishes self-government. If Congress uses the opposition it aroused to overthrow antiquity and introduce equality, antiquity and capital roar, and set the Congress dependent upon them against the reformers. That is what now occurs, and why the attack falls upon shirts of every colour.

The condition will grow. Many forces compete to enjoy the benefits of self-rule. At this great moment, anyone anywhere, whatever name they use, who holds a vigorous policy scented with equality in social, religious and economic affairs will be detected and attacked. Whether the attack succeeds is another matter; history says such attacks ultimately shatter the attackers. But orthodoxy and capitalism now roam, sniffing through every town for radicals and advocates of new methods.

This evil force seeking to prevent a new awakening will grow at fever speed and fall through that very growth. Radicals now need one thing: strength to endure the assault. If radicalism is extinguished in this generation, the thought of what follows makes one tremble; the country will fall into antiquity's terrible grip.

### Before Pandit Nehru's Eyes

The average income of the poor man in this country is only two annas. How can he live comfortably or raise his standard of life? Must he not suffer even for half a stomach of gruel? Leaders said this misery resulted from imperialism. Once its grip ended and government passed to national leaders, life would flourish and they could act without interference. They still say so, and people naturally expect it. The fault is not theirs: powerful propaganda created and continues to create this confidence.

British rule did not develop sufficient industry here. The foreign ruler did not establish industries for this country's welfare; he used political power for his own country's prosperity and prevented local capital from developing new production. Wealth could not grow, output did not rise with population, and people died of hunger and famine.

Leaders had proclaimed that once the Briton departed, scarcity and poverty would vanish and this “holy land” become a magnificent place of abundance. The people accepted those promises completely because of the confidence inspired by skilful speeches.

As industry failed to grow, unemployment increased. Rather than starve at home, people crossed the sea to work as servants in tea estates and rubber forests. Food initially satisfied those beaten by famine, as shade delights one arriving from the sun, but peace did not last. Separation from their birthplace, fear of the future, declining life and longing to return left them without dignity abroad. They were told that once imperialism was shattered and rule passed to patriots, all could return, make their country golden and live with honour. How could they fail to believe?

The grip has loosened. Famous patriots occupy office, while the Briton remains merely an agent and guard, and even that will not last long. But are the leaders' promises and the people's expectations being fulfilled? Are suitable plans being prepared? Every question receives the same answer: no.

The rulers' course neither feeds the hungry nor employs the jobless. Their thought is directed toward preserving capitalist prestige and profit, capturing markets in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, confronting competition, leading Asia, building an army and guarding national honour.

The poor once lived in darkness under white capitalists. Now northern capitalists' frenzy further scorches their barren lives. Though the imperial seat changed and patriots occupied it, no transformation brought happiness to the poor. Capitalist desire for profit is concealed behind the curtain of sacrifice and presented as noble policy.

Many scholars besides us warned that this condition would arise, but public illusion kept their words unheard. It is not yet too late. Awakened people can forget former likes and dislikes, unite, restrain this dangerous course and compel plans that benefit everyone. It is difficult but possible.

We do not criticize merely to find fault. We say with evidence that the rulers' policy will further impoverish the people. A few days ago Finance Minister Shanmukham introduced in the Indian Parliament a bill to create an Industrial Finance Corporation to finance basic industries.

What is its nature? Who will profit, capitalists or people? Why is it needed? Its total capital is five crore rupees, divided into two thousand shares of Rs. 25,000 each. The Indian Government and Reserve Bank receive four hundred shares each; the remaining twelve hundred go to capitalists and financial institutions under their control, including banks and insurance companies. The Government guarantees repayment of the others' capital and a stated return upon it.

The corporation may issue at least twenty crore rupees in bonds, with government guaranteeing principal and interest; accept up to ten crore rupees in long-term deposits from anyone; and, with Central Government permission, borrow abroad.

Eleven people administer it: two representing government, three representing a Reserve Bank itself governed by industrial magnates, and five representing capitalist-controlled banks and insurance companies. Though government appoints the managing director, it does so only after consulting Reserve Bank directors.

Given the sources of capital and method of appointing directors, is it not clear that the Industrial Finance Corporation can only be a first-rate institution dominated by capitalists? Government supervision and control are decorative words.

Introducing the bill, Finance Minister Shanmukham said the corporation was established specifically to assist industries begun by private individuals. Capitalism throughout the world faces a great crisis. Capitalist countries and Indian capitalists know it. Shanmukham, himself one of them, recognizes the coming danger and has undertaken the great labour of protecting his class. This is not our invention; his own words prove it.

He explained that whatever the financial strength of a private industrial concern, a stage would inevitably arise when it needed substantial assistance that could not ordinarily be obtained from the public, and that its survival and growth would depend upon such aid. The corporation was being created to save industries from such crises.

Thus Shanmukham clearly knows that capitalism has no future, that crisis approaches, that private capitalists cannot resolve it independently, and that government must rescue them. He has undertaken this sacred task to save his partners. We are not surprised, because we know that the castor fruit is not sweet.

Even after capitalists learn that their system is useless, they will not voluntarily cast it away. Those who preached that equality was the cure and the ideal of their lives were duty-bound to hasten capitalism's end and build a better order. Yet only in this “holy land” can they shut their eyes, remain silent, allow what should be plucked out with a fingernail to grow, and say “so be it.”

Before the very eyes of Pandit Nehru, who deafened us with talk of equality and proclaimed it his ideal, efforts are made to revive capitalism. Instead of preparing for struggle, the socialist Pandit says “so be it” to Shanmukham's course.

## Translator's Notes

The title-page date is clearly `13-7-1946` in the scan. Tamil OCR misread it as `73-7-1946`; the English translation uses the scan-verified date while the verbatim Tamil source block remains unchanged.

Part 1, Image 9 is a table scanned sideways. Its Tamil OCR is unusable, so the English table above was manually transcribed from the scan. Several bank names are difficult to identify with certainty from the Tamil type; transliterations are deliberately conservative and should be checked against contemporary English banking records. The numerical cells were read directly from the scan.

The Tamil OCR source remains unchanged.

The company lists in Part 3 contain numerous noisy OCR readings of names and capital figures. The translation preserves their economic scope and categories without silently asserting uncertain English spellings. Those tables require image-level transcription before final publication.

Part 5, Image 5 is a sideways table whose OCR is unusable. Direct scan inspection confirms the ten leading companies' net funds range from Oriental's Rs. 35 crore 83 lakh to Indian Mercantile General's Rs. 2 crore 42 lakh, as described in the prose, and confirms the income and surplus columns. Several managing-agent names remain typographically uncertain and are therefore not silently normalized. The OCR's aggregate assets, liabilities and net-assets figures contain an arithmetic conflict and have not been silently reconciled.

Part 6's ninety-name maritime-trader list is retained verbatim in the Tamil source. Because many proper names are OCR-uncertain, the English translation describes the evidentiary list rather than silently inventing normalized spellings. The English quotation in Part 6, Image 9 was recovered directly from the scan and reproduced verbatim, including its original capitalization and punctuation.
